text(await tools.apply_patch("*** Begin Patch\n*** Add File: students/amanda/recaps/2026-09-22-recap.md\n+---\n+title: Recap · Amanda · 2026-09-22\n+type: recap\n+record_kind: primary\n+student: amanda\n+session_date: 2026-09-22\n+source: transcript-downloads/2026-09-22-amanda-notes-by-gemini.md\n+status: draft\n+posted: false\n+posted_at:\n+---\n+\n+# Conditional Inferences: Keep the Direction and the Details\n+\n+A conditional rule tells you what follows when its trigger is met. The key is to identify the two complete ideas, put them on the right sides of the arrow, and keep every qualification attached. Then you can connect rules or apply a stated fact without giving the rule more power than it has.\n+\n+## Build the rule before making an inference\n+\n+When a sentence feels dense, take it in this order:\n+\n+1. Find words that signal a conditional relationship, such as *if*, *only if*, *when*, *always*, or *never*.\n+2. State the two ideas in ordinary English. Resolve references such as *they* so you know exactly which words the rule concerns.\n+3. Place the sufficient condition, the trigger, on the left. Place what the rule guarantees on the right.\n+4. Write the contrapositive by reversing the two sides and negating both.\n+5. Ask what the rule **does not** tell you. That check catches the tempting reversal and inverse.\n+\n+For example:\n+\n+```text\n+If a word is the first, last, or a main word of a title,\n+then it should be capitalized.\n+\n+First/last/main → should capitalize\n+Should not capitalize → not first/last/main\n+```\n+\n+The rule does **not** say that every other word should remain lowercase. A sufficient condition can guarantee an outcome without being the only route to it. The rule also does not say that a word *was* capitalized. *Should* describes the correct treatment, not what someone actually typed.\n+\n+## Keep the qualification inside the condition\n+\n+Suppose a second rule says that an article, preposition, or conjunction **with fewer than five letters**, when it appears in the middle of a title, should never be capitalized. The word class, length, and position all matter. Do not turn that into “prepositions are never capitalized.”\n+\n+```text\n+Middle of title + short article/preposition/conjunction\n+    → should not capitalize\n+\n+Should capitalize\n+    → not a middle-of-title short article/preposition/conjunction\n+```\n+\n+“Short” means fewer than five letters here. A preposition of five or more letters does not satisfy this rule's trigger. If an answer leaves out the length or the position, it may assert more than the rule allows.\n+\n+Notice how the two title rules can connect. A first, last, or main word **should** be capitalized. Under the second rule's contrapositive, something that should be capitalized cannot be a middle-of-title short article, preposition, or conjunction. The repeated idea, *should capitalize*, is the connection. Keep the word *should* throughout; replacing it with *is capitalized* changes the claim.\n+\n+## Know whether you are using facts or joining rules\n+\n+A rule does not say its trigger occurred. In a Must Be True question, you may be given a fact that activates a rule, or you may be given several rules and no triggering fact.\n+\n+| What the stimulus gives you | Legal move |\n+|---|---|\n+| A rule and a matching fact | Apply the rule to that fact. |\n+| Several rules with a shared condition | Connect the rules and infer the relationship between the endpoints. |\n+| A fact matching the rule's necessary side | No inference about its sufficient side follows from that alone. |\n+\n+For example, “If someone burned the building, they are guilty of arson” gives `burned building → guilty of arson`. Its contrapositive is `not guilty of arson → did not burn building`. A fact that someone is not guilty of arson supports the second inference. It does not show that the person is not guilty of *any* crime.\n+\n+With multiple rules, connect them as you read:\n+\n+```text\n+A → B\n+B → C\n+Therefore A → C, and not C → not A.\n+```\n+\n+The repeated middle idea is the point of connection. The two endpoints are often what the answer tests. But the wording may change between sentences, so check that the supposedly repeated idea really means the same thing before joining the arrows. A matching letter on paper cannot replace an understanding of the sentence.\n+\n+## Predict before reading the rest of a conditional answer\n+\n+When an answer begins “If X…,” pause before its “then” clause. Ask what your diagram actually guarantees from X. Say that prediction in plain English, then read the remainder of the answer and compare it. This prevents the answer's wording from persuading you that a relationship exists when it was never established.\n+\n+Check the boundary as well as the direction. An answer can have the right-looking arrow while dropping *fewer than five letters*, changing *should* to *does*, or treating “not first, last, or main” as if it guaranteed “should not capitalize.”\n+\n+## A repeatable process\n+\n+1. Translate each sentence into two complete ideas and identify the trigger.\n+2. Diagram the original rule and its contrapositive. Keep qualifiers attached to the condition they limit.\n+3. As soon as another rule repeats an idea, check whether the wording truly matches and, if so, join the chain.\n+4. Label any stated facts separately. Activate a rule only when a fact satisfies its trigger.\n+5. Before accepting an answer, compare its exact direction, scope, and wording with what the chain proves.\n+\n+## Before next session\n+\n+- [ ] Complete the conditional-logic and chaining practice Joshua assigns on the study platform. Focus on diagramming, contrapositives, and connecting rules as you read.\n+\n+---\n+\n+## Notes for me (not posted)\n+\n+- **Weak spots:** Familiarity with conditional terms without reflexive accuracy; initial inversion of the first title rule; keeping “fewer than five letters” attached to the word classes; resolving “they” in the second rule; connecting conditionals in context.\n+- **Follow-ups on my side:** Assign the promised conditional drills and put the recap on the study platform when separately authorized. Send Amanda the accommodations form and the discussed accommodations list, plus a scheduling link. Amanda said she would take the form to a healthcare professional at her 2026-09-23 appointment. Confirm any form content and deadline from the official source before relying on the session's spoken claims.\n+- **Watch:** Joshua intended to hold off on an additional recall drill and concentrate the next work on conditionals. The Gemini document says Sep 23, while the Calendar session is 2026-09-22 at 20:00 EDT, which is 2026-09-23 00:00 UTC; the date difference likely reflects time zone handling.\n*** End Patch"))

text(await tools.apply_patch("*** Begin Patch\n*** Add File: students/sofia/recaps/2026-09-23-recap.md\n+---\n+title: Recap · Sofia · 2026-09-23\n+type: recap\n+record_kind: primary\n+student: sofia\n+session_date: 2026-09-23\n+source: transcript-downloads/2026-09-23-sofia-notes-by-gemini.md\n+status: draft\n+posted: false\n+posted_at:\n+---\n+\n+# Sufficient Assumptions: Find the Missing Link\n+\n+When a conclusion is conditional, diagramming the premises is only the first step. A sufficient assumption must complete a route from the conclusion's sufficient side to its necessary side. Keep the conclusion separate from the evidence until the answer supplies that route.\n+\n+## Separate the claim from the support\n+\n+Suppose an author concludes:\n+\n+```text\n+If a democracy can thrive, it has effective news media.\n+Thrive → effective media\n+```\n+\n+That arrow is the claim to be proved. You cannot place it among the established rules merely because the author wrote it. The premises might establish only:\n+\n+```text\n+Thrive → knowledgeable electorate\n+Knowledgeable electorate → access to unbiased information\n+```\n+\n+Together, the premises show `thrive → access to unbiased information`. They still do not show `thrive → effective media`. The path stops at unbiased information. The missing link is what would take that endpoint to effective media.\n+\n+One sufficient answer would give:\n+\n+```text\n+Access to unbiased information → effective media\n+```\n+\n+Then the entire route works:\n+\n+```text\n+Thrive → knowledgeable electorate → access to unbiased information\n+       → effective media\n+```\n+\n+An answer can express the same link as its contrapositive: `not effective media → no access to unbiased information`. Reverse and negate both sides to check it. Do not merely reverse the arrow.\n+\n+## Use both halves of the conclusion to locate the gap\n+\n+Start with the conclusion's two sides. Ask which side appears in the premises and how far its path goes. Here, *thrive* is the starting point in both the conclusion and the evidence. The evidence extends it through knowledge to unbiased information. The other side, *effective media*, has no established connection to that chain.\n+\n+Your prediction is therefore specific: the answer must connect something on the established path to effective media. It might connect the last endpoint, unbiased information, or an earlier point such as a knowledgeable electorate. A link from unbiased information is the most direct route. In every case, test the completed chain from the conclusion's left side all the way to its right side.\n+\n+This prevents a subtle mistake: treating the conclusion's own arrow as evidence. If the only place two ideas are linked is the sentence the author is trying to prove, the argument still has a gap.\n+\n+## Sufficient Assumption and Must Be True use the same skill differently\n+\n+| Question | Your job |\n+|---|---|\n+| Sufficient Assumption | Add a rule that makes the stated conclusion follow from the premises. |\n+| Must Be True | Use the given rules and facts to find a conclusion that already follows. |\n+\n+With `A → B` and `B → C` in the premises, a Must Be True answer can state `A → C`. If the argument *concludes* `A → C` but supplies only `A → B`, a Sufficient Assumption answer could add `B → C`. The arrows are familiar in both tasks; the difference is whether you are extracting a result or supplying the connection needed to prove one.\n+\n+## Make the arrows answer a real question\n+\n+A diagram represents what the words mean. It is not a reason to skip their meaning. “Specialized training is required to become a literature professor” means `literature professor → specialized training`. It does not mean `specialized training → literature professor`. The ability to judge literary greatness may also require specialized training. Two routes to the same necessary condition do not establish a relationship between their starting points.\n+\n+```text\n+Judge greatness → specialized training\n+Literature professor → specialized training\n+```\n+\n+From these two arrows alone, you cannot infer that everyone who can judge greatness is a professor, or that everyone who is not a professor cannot judge greatness. But if a person lacks specialized training, the contrapositives do let you rule out both activities that require it. Check each conclusion against an actual path in the correct direction.\n+\n+## A repeatable process\n+\n+1. Identify the conclusion and diagram its sufficient and necessary sides. Set that arrow aside as the claim to prove.\n+2. Diagram the premises in ordinary language. Join only arrows whose conditions truly match.\n+3. Follow the premise chain from the conclusion's sufficient side. Mark where it stops.\n+4. Predict the missing link from that stopping point to the conclusion's necessary side. Check the answer in both its original form and its contrapositive.\n+5. Run the complete chain. If it reaches the conclusion's necessary side without adding any other assumption, the answer is sufficient.\n+\n+## Before next session\n+\n+- [ ] Finish the remaining half of the conditional-logic boot camp, especially its linking and application work.\n+- [ ] Complete the sufficient-assumption boot camp once Joshua assigns it.\n+- [ ] Work through a Logical Reasoning section and review the missed questions, looking closely at conditional links and where a chain stops.\n+\n+---\n+\n+## Notes for me (not posted)\n+\n+- **Weak spots:** Sofia often diagrams the premises correctly but does not use the chain to locate the missing link in a sufficient assumption. She can be drawn to an attractive answer after a correct setup. In the literature-professor item, the two arrows shared a necessary condition and did not form the A-to-C chain she initially made.\n+- **Follow-ups on my side:** Assign the sufficient-assumption boot camp Joshua said he created. He also promised to review Sofia's recent sections and make a targeted study strategy. Sofia said she would finish the conditional boot camp, work through a section, review wrong answers, and check the schedule.\n+- **Watch:** Section practice with immediate review was favored over repeated full tests as the primary use of study time. The transcript after approximately 00:50:16 shifts to an unrelated conversation with another person and is excluded from this recap.\n*** End Patch"))

## Tool: exec

text(await tools.apply_patch("*** Begin Patch\n*** Add File: students/sandra/recaps/2026-09-23-recap.md\n+---\n+title: Recap · Sandra · 2026-09-23\n+type: recap\n+record_kind: primary\n+student: sandra\n+session_date: 2026-09-23\n+source: transcript-downloads/2026-09-23-sandra-notes-by-gemini.md\n+status: draft\n+posted: false\n+posted_at:\n+---\n+\n+# Intermediate Conclusions: Follow the Support to the Main Claim\n+\n+An intermediate conclusion is a claim the author supports and then uses to support a further claim. Its position comes from the job it does in the argument, not from whether it sounds important or appears near the end. Finding it helps you identify the true main conclusion and see exactly where an argument's reasoning may fail.\n+\n+## See the three levels of an argument\n+\n+An argument links evidence to a claim. When it has an intermediate conclusion, the support runs through two links:\n+\n+```text\n+Premise → intermediate conclusion → main conclusion\n+```\n+\n+For example:\n+\n+```text\n+He still plays at an age when most players have retired.\n+    ↓ supports\n+He is the most dominant player of the modern era.\n+    ↓ supports\n+He is the greatest player of all time.\n+```\n+\n+The middle statement is a conclusion because the first statement supports it. It is also support because the author uses it as a reason for the final claim. If the final claim were removed, the middle statement could become the main conclusion of the shorter argument. Its role depends on the whole chain.\n+\n+## Ask “Why should I believe this?” twice\n+\n+Take a candidate main conclusion and ask: “Why should I believe this?” If another statement answers, you have found support. Then ask the same question of that supporting statement. If a third statement answers, the one in the middle is an intermediate conclusion.\n+\n+Consider:\n+\n+1. People who value wealth often choose better-paid work even when the location is less desirable.\n+2. What people value is strongly related to how they act.\n+3. Knowing a person's values can help predict their actions.\n+\n+Why believe statement 3? Because values and actions are related, as statement 2 says. Why believe statement 2? Statement 1 gives a concrete example of that relationship. The order of support is `1 → 2 → 3`, even if the statements appear in a different order on the page.\n+\n+Test the reverse direction when two claims both sound like conclusions. “We can predict actions because values and actions correlate” gives a reason. “Values and actions correlate because we can predict actions” does not give the same support. The better support relationship tells you which conclusion is intermediate and which is main.\n+\n+## Let indicator words guide you, then verify the relationship\n+\n+*Because*, *since*, *for*, and *after all* often introduce a premise. *So*, *thus*, and *therefore* often introduce a conclusion. *But* often signals a turn in the author's position. Use those words when they appear, but still ask what the statements do for one another. An argument can omit indicators or use one indicator for only part of its structure.\n+\n+Read references and changes in position carefully. “Many people assume conflicts are inevitable, **but** that assumption is false” means the author concludes conflicts are **not** inevitable. A later explanation that conflicts arise largely from irrational judgments can support that main claim. Examples of judging one bad act harshly while requiring years of good conduct to trust someone support the intermediate claim about irrationality.\n+\n+```text\n+Uneven judgments about others\n+    → people often act irrationally in conflicts\n+    → conflicts are not inevitable\n+```\n+\n+The examples are not the main point. The claim about irrationality is supported by them and supports the author's larger conclusion.\n+\n+## Keep an “if” from turning into a recommendation\n+\n+Small wording changes matter when you compare an answer with a conclusion. “**If** people in modern communities cooperated on shared tasks, their communities would probably become closer” describes a predicted result under a condition. It does not say that people **should** undertake those tasks. An answer that turns the condition into a recommendation adds a claim the author did not make.\n+\n+Once you find the main conclusion, mark it in the stimulus. Then compare each candidate answer directly with its wording. This removes the need to hold the sentence in memory while reading the choices. Check whether the answer preserves qualifications such as *if*, *probably*, and *some*, as well as the author's actual claim.\n+\n+## Use the structure to find a reasoning gap\n+\n+There are two links to examine in a three-level argument:\n+\n+1. Does the premise support the intermediate conclusion?\n+2. Does the intermediate conclusion support the main conclusion?\n+\n+An argument can be weak at either link. Identifying the intermediate conclusion lets you say precisely where the support stops. This matters beyond main-conclusion questions because many other questions ask you to assess the argument's reasoning.\n+\n+## A repeatable process\n+\n+1. Tell back each statement in plain words. Resolve phrases such as “this process” or “that assumption.”\n+2. Mark indicator words, if present. Treat them as clues about support, not substitutes for understanding.\n+3. Ask “Why should I believe this?” of the candidate main claim, then of the statement that supports it.\n+4. Label the statement that both receives and gives support as the intermediate conclusion.\n+5. Mark the main conclusion and compare answers against it, checking direction, strength, and any conditional wording.\n+\n+## Before next session\n+\n+- [ ] Complete the intermediate-conclusion question set Joshua said he would assign. For each argument, identify the premise, intermediate conclusion, and main conclusion, then test both support links.\n+\n+---\n+\n+## Notes for me (not posted)\n+\n+- **Weak spots:** Sandra is quicker at finding main conclusions and said she misses some questions through incomplete reading. She wanted a practical way to distinguish two conclusions, asked why identifying the intermediate one matters, and was unsure how much to rely on indicator words. The “so” indicator was initially mistaken for a premise indicator.\n+- **Follow-ups on my side:** Assign the promised intermediate-conclusion LSAT questions. Add the postponed session for Monday and reconcile the other moved sessions Joshua mentioned. He expected a session the following day to review the assigned work.\n+- **Watch:** She benefited from marking the main conclusion and checking answer wording directly. Continue practicing both support links even when a main-conclusion answer appears obvious.\n*** End Patch"))
